Transonic shock solutions for steady 3-d axisymmetric full Euler flows with large swirl velocity in a finite cylindrical nozzle
Beixiang Fang, Xin Gao, Wei Xiang, Qin Zhao

Abstract
. This paper concerns the existence and location of three-dimensional axisymmetric transonic shocks with large swirl velocity for shock solutions of the steady compressible full Euler system in a cylindrical nozzle with prescribed receiver pressure. As far as we know, it is the first mathematical result on the three-dimensional transonic shock with either large vorticity or large swirl velocity. One of the key difficulties is the fact that the Euler system is elliptic-hyperbolic composite for the flow behind the shock front, and its elliptic part and hyperbolic part are strongly coupled in the lower order terms because of the large swirl velocity, such that they cannot be simply decoupled in the principal parts as the case for the flow without swirls or with small swirl velocity. New decomposition techniques for the elliptic-hyperbolic composite system are developed to deal with this difficulty, and the solvability condition for the boundary value problem is deduced to determine the location of the shock front. It finally turns out that the non-zero swirl velocity, which brings new challenging difficulties in the analysis, plays an essential and fundamental role in determining the location of the shock front. Another key difficulty in the analysis is that there are no readily established shock solutions available, which is different from the flows without swirls, for which trivial piecewise constant shock solutions can be easily constructed. Non-trivial special shock solutions are first constructed as background solutions under the assumption that the flow parameters depend only on the radial distance to the symmetric axis. Necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of such solutions are also found. Even though the shock location can be arbitrarily shifted for the special shock solutions, it will be shown that the shock solution with a determined shock position can be established as the boundary data are perturbations of one of the established special shock solutions under certain conditions.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35A02, 35B20, 35B35, 35B65, 35J56, 35L65, 35L67, 35M30, 35M32, 35Q31, 35R35, 76L05, 76N10

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we are concerned with the existence and shock location of the transonic shock solutions for steady compressible three-dimensional (3-d) axisymmetric full Euler flows with large vorticity via large swirl velocity in a finite cylindrical nozzle with prescribed receiver pressure at the exit (see Figure
1.1
).
The steady compressible 3-d Euler flow is governed by the following system

(1.1)

\displaystyle\di(\rho\mathbf{u})=0,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\di(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})+\nabla p=0,

(1.3)

\displaystyle\di(\rho(e+\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{p}{\rho})\mathbf{u})=0,

where
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
are the space variables, “
\di
” is the divergence operator with respect to
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})^{\top}
is the velocity field,
\rho
,
p
and
e
stand for the density, pressure, and the internal energy respectively.
Moreover, the fluid is assumed to be polytropic, which satisfies the following thermal relation

p=Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}{\rho}^{\gamma},

where
s
is the entropy,
\gamma>1
is the adiabatic exponent,
c_{v}
is the specific heat at constant volume and
A
is a positive constant.

It is a longstanding open problem to establish the existence of transonic shock solutions to the system (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) in a finite 3-d nozzle and catch the location of the shock front under the boundary conditions proposed by Courant-Friedrichs in
[
11
]
as follows:

For given supersonic states at the nozzle entry and receiver pressure at the nozzle exit, try to determine the steady flow pattern with a single transonic shock front, including the location of the shock front as well as the states of the flow ahead of and behind it, in a finite 3-d nozzle, satisfying the slip boundary condition on the nozzle wall.

Therefore, roughly speaking, we will impose the following boundary conditions in this paper:

(1.4)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{all incoming states at the entrance,}\\
\mbox{the slip boundary condition on the nozzle wall,}\\
\mbox{and receiver pressure at the outlet.}\end{array}

The shock problem is mathematically formulated as a free boundary problem to the Euler system (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) under the boundary conditions (
1.4
) with prescribed data, which will be specified in detail in Section 2.
The formulated free boundary problem is a strong nonlinear problem, and the location of the shock front is the very free boundary to be determined together with the solution.
In this paper, the flow is assumed to swirl around the symmetric axis with a large speed, and we are going to determine the location of the shock front under the prescribed boundary data and establish the existence of the shock solution to the formulated free boundary problem.

Figure 1.1.
Transonic shock flow with large swirl velocity in a cylinder.

Even though there has been lots of literature on the existence of steady axisymmetric transonic shock solutions with zero-swirl velocity or with sufficiently small swirl velocity in a 3-d cylindrical nozzle (for instance, see
[
14
,
26
,
27
,
36
,
37
,
35
,
38
]
and references therein),
as far as we know, there is no rigorous result on the transonic flow with general swirl velocity.
In fact, it is still an open problem whether there exist shock solutions with general swirl velocity under the boundary conditions proposed by Courant-Friedrichs in
[
11
]
, and how we determine the admissible shock locations if such shock solutions exist.
This paper is devoted to this problem by establishing the existence of axisymmetric shock solutions to the system (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) with large swirl velocity in a finite cylindrical nozzle (see Figure
1.1
), where the location of the shock front is determined by the boundary data (
1.4
).
In this paper, two major steps will be carried out as follows to deal with key difficulties and establish the existence of the shock solution with a determined location of the shock front.

\underline{Step\ 1:}

The first step is to establish necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of special shock solutions to the system (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) with large swirl velocity under the assumption that the states of the flow both ahead of and behind the shock front depend only on the radial distance to the symmetry axis.

One difficulty arising immediately from the large swirl velocity is that there is no readily established background shock solution available, which is different from the flows without swirls, for which trivial piecewise constant shock solutions can be easily established.
Such shock solutions cannot be trivially established because, across the shock front, the flow may fail to satisfy the boundary conditions proposed above and the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions derived from the Euler system (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) simultaneously.
Moreover, the shock front location of these special solutions can be arbitrarily shifted in the nozzle, so that it cannot be used as a background solution directly.

\underline{Step\ 2:}

Based on the special shock solutions established in
Step
1
, the second step is devoted to determining the location of the shock front and establishing the existence of the shock solution to the system (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) for steady axisymmetric flows under small perturbation of the states at the nozzle entry as well as the receiver pressure at the exit.

•

Since the location of the shock front for the special shock solution can be arbitrarily shifted, we have to determine the location of the shock front for the perturbed flow directly from the perturbed boundary data.
Actually, it is a longstanding mathematical challenge to find plausible mechanisms to determine the location of the shock front.

•

Because of the large swirl velocity, the elliptic part and the hyperbolic part of the Euler system for the flow behind the shock front are strongly coupled in the lower order terms, so that they cannot simply be decoupled in the principal parts as the case for the flow without swirl velocity or with small swirl velocity.
Hence, classical theory for elliptic PDEs and hyperbolic PDEs cannot be directly employed to determine the location of the shock front and solve the free boundary problem.

•

The singularity along the symmetry axis brings additional difficulties in solving the free boundary problem.

New decomposition techniques for the Euler system will be developed in this paper to deal with these difficulties. The solvability conditions and approximate solutions for the boundary value problem of the elliptic-hyperbolic composite system will be derived. It turns out that, for the perturbation problem in this step, the non-zero swirl velocity of the unperturbed shock solution plays an essential role and contributes the key terms in determining the location of the shock front.
This provides an alternative mechanism, different from the one observed in
[
20
]
, where the geometry of the nozzle boundary plays a key role.

To overcome these challenging difficulties, in
Step
1
, we find that the Mach number function of the incoming flow ahead of the shock front plays an important role, and the special shock solutions can be constructed if and only if the Mach number function satisfies certain conditions.
In
Step
2
, approximate shock solutions (see §
6.1
) are constructed to capture the location of the shock front directly from the perturbed boundary data.
The key step is to solve the free boundary problem of the steady Euler system for the flow behind the shock front, which is an elliptic-hyperbolic composite system.
However, the large swirl velocity of the unperturbed flow, strongly coupled nonlinear lower-order terms, and the singularity along the symmetric axis bring new challenging difficulties that the methods and techniques developed in
[
20
,
14
]
for the shock problem for the flow without swirl velocity cannot deal with.

As far as we know, there is no general mathematical theory for such problems, and we have to develop techniques dealing with them.
By observing that the deduced elliptic-hyperbolic composite system has special structural properties, we develop a partly-decouple technique by introducing two auxiliary problems described as follows.
One auxiliary problem is a boundary value problem of an elliptic system of first order, which is reduced from the elliptic part of the whole composite system by taking out the coupling
0
-th order terms.
In order to make sure this problem admits a solution, the boundary data should satisfy a solvability condition.
This solvability condition, where the large swirling velocity contributes essentially, serves to catch the location of the shock front.
It shows a different mechanism in determining the location of the shock front from the ones in
[
20
,
14
]
.
The other auxiliary problem is reduced by subtracting the obtained solution to the previous auxiliary problem from the problem for the whole composite system with the strongly coupled non-local terms.
Techniques and methods are developed to deal with the difficulty coming from these coupling non-local terms such that the unique existence of the solution as well as the
a priori
estimates can be established.

For the singularity along the symmetry axis, firstly, the solution to the steady Euler system for the flow ahead of the shock front cannot be directly considered as readily solved by applying the classical theory of hyperbolic systems because of the singularities along the symmetric axis, which is a part of the boundary.
Techniques are developed to solve initial-boundary value problems of hyperbolic systems with singularities on a part of the boundary.
Moreover, the singularity along the symmetry axis also brings difficulties for both auxiliary problems introduced above to deal with the boundary value problem of the elliptic-hyperbolic system for the flow behind the shock front.
We develop a method by considering the problems in domains of higher dimensions to overcome them.
Careful estimates are established to deal with the singularities of the solution along the symmetric axis.

Thanks to steady efforts made by many mathematicians, there have been plenty of results on gas flows with shocks in nozzles, for instance, see
[
1
,
8
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
9
,
10
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
20
,
19
,
18
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
31
,
30
,
36
,
37
,
35
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
44
,
45
]
and references therein.
For steady multi-dimensional flows with a shock in nozzles, in order to determine the position of the shock front, Courant and Friedrichs pointed out in
[
11
]
that, without rigorous mathematical analysis, additional conditions are needed to be imposed at the exit of the nozzle and the pressure condition is preferred among different possible options.
From then on, many mathematicians have been working on this issue and there have been many substantial progresses.
In particular, Chen-Feldman proved in
[
4
]
the existence of transonic shock solutions in a finite flat
nozzle for multi-dimensional potential flows with given potential value at the exit and an assumption that the shock front passes through a given point. Later in
[
3
]
, with given vertical component of the velocity at the exit, Chen-Chen-Song established the existence of the shock solutions for 2-d steady Euler flows. Both existence results are established under the assumption that the shock front passes through a given point.
Without this assumption and with a given pressure at the exit, recently in
[
20
]
, Fang-Xin established the existence of the transonic shock solutions in an almost flat (but non-flat) nozzle. It is interesting that the results in
[
20
]
indicate that, in a flat nozzle and for given pressure condition at the exit, there may exist more than one admissible positions of the shock front. So it is still open to determine the shock location in a flat nozzle. This is one of the main reason that we work on the flat nozzle in this paper.
For the problems in a diverging nozzle,
the position of the shock front can be uniquely determined for a given pressure at the exit under the assumption that the flow states depend only on the radius (see
[
11
]
). And the structural stability of this shock solution
has been established for 2-d case in
[
9
]
by Chen and in
[
25
,
28
]
by Li-Xin-Yin.
For 3-d axisymmetric case, in
[
38
]
, Weng-Xie-Xin proved the structure stability of the transonic shock solutions under a small perturbation of the swirl velocity; in
[
37
]
, Park proved the structure stability under a small perturbation of the supersonic flow. Finally, Fang-Gao in
[
14
]
established the existence of the 3-d axisymmetric flow in a small perturbed cylinder with zero-swirl velocity. See also
[
12
,
13
]
for the 3-d compressible axisymmetric smooth Euler flow with non-zero swirl velocity in a nozzle and
[
24
]
for the stability of two dimentional transonic contact discontinuity in a finite nozzle.

Finally, for convenience of the reader, we briefly describe the structure of the paper.
In Section 2, the mathematical formulation of the problem for the existence of shock solutions for steady compressible 3-d axisymmetric full Euler flows with non-zero swirl velocity will be described.
Then the previously mentioned two steps, which will be carried out in this paper to establish the existence of the shock solutions with determined location of the shock front, will be described with more details and main theorems will be stated.
Section 3 is devoted to carrying out
Step 1
to establish the existence and uniqueness of special shock solutions with non-zero swirl velocity.
Moreover, the properties of these established solutions will also be described.
Based on the special solutions established in Section 3, the remaining sections are devoted to carrying out
Step 2
.
Several preliminary theorems will be firstly established in Section 4 and Section 5, which deal with the previously mentioned challenging difficulties in
Step 2
.
More precisely,
in Section 4,
we develop techniques to solve the initial-boundary value problem of the hyperbolic system with singularities on a part of the boundary, and then establish the existence of the solution for the axisymmetric Euler flow ahead of the shock front.
In Section 5,
we develop techniques to deal with the boundary value problem of the elliptic-hyperbolic composite system deduced from the linearized Euler system for the flow behind the shock front and establishes the existence of the solutions to the associating linearized problems.
With the help of the preliminary theorems in Section 4 and Section 5, we establish the existence of the shock solution with determined location of the shock front for the axisymmetric Euler flow with large swirl velocity under certain sufficient conditions and conclude the proof of the main theorem in this paper in Section 6.

2.
Mathematical formulation and main theorems

In this section, we first formulate the transonic shock problem in a finite cylinder as a free boundary problem for the steady 3-d axisymmetric full Euler flows.
Then we state several lemmas, theorems and finally the main theorem one by one.

2.1.
Free boundary value problem for 3-d steady axisymmetric full Euler flows in a finite cylinder

Let the nozzle be defined by

(2.1)

\mathcal{N}:=\left\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}:0<x_{1}<L,\ 0\leq x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}<r_{0}^{2}\right\},

with the entrance
\mathcal{N}_{en}
, the exit
\mathcal{N}_{ex}
, and the nozzle wall
\mathcal{N}_{w}
(see Figure
1.1
)

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{en}&:=\overline{\mathcal{N}}\cap\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}:x_{1}=0\},\\
\mathcal{N}_{ex}&:=\overline{\mathcal{N}}\cap\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}:x_{1}=L\},\\
\mathcal{N}_{w}&:=\overline{\mathcal{N}}\cap\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}:x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}=r_{0}^{2}\}.\end{split}

Let
(z,r,\varpi)
be the cylindrical coordinate in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that

\displaystyle(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(z,r\cos\varpi,r\sin\varpi).

Then the domain
\mathcal{N}
and its boundaries can be expressed in
(z,r)
-coordinates as

(2.2)

\Omega:=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0<z<L,\ 0\leq r<r_{0}\}

and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{en}=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:z=0,\,0\leq r<r_{0}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0<z<L,\,r=0\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{ex}=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:z=L,\,0\leq r<r_{0}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{w}=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0<z<L,\,r=r_{0}\}.

In the cylinder
\mathcal{N}
, we expect the flow is axisymmetric, that is, if we define

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle:=u_{1},

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle:=u_{2}\cos\varpi+u_{3}\sin\varpi,

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle:=u_{3}\cos\varpi-u_{2}\sin\varpi,

then
(u,v,w)
and
(p,\rho)
are independent on
\varpi
. Here
w
is called the swirl velocity.
Then the steady 3-d full Euler system (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) becomes the following steady axisymmetric Euler system for
(u,v,w,p,\rho)
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}(r\rho u)+\partial_{r}(r\rho v)=0,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}(p+\rho u^{2})+\partial_{r}(\rho uv)+\frac{\rho uv}{r}=0,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}(\rho uv)+\partial_{r}(p+\rho v^{2})+\frac{\rho(v^{2}-w^{2})}{r}=0,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}(\rho uw)+\partial_{r}(\rho vw)+2\frac{\rho vw}{r}=0,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\Big(r\rho u(\frac{1}{2}(q^{2}+w^{2})+i)\Big)+\partial_{r}\Big(r\rho v(\frac{1}{2}(q^{2}+w^{2})+i)\Big)=0,

where

q^{2}:=u^{2}+v^{2}
and
i:=\displaystyle\frac{\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)\rho}
are the speed modulo the swirl velocity and enthalpy, respectively.
Because the flow is axisymmetric, we know that

(2.8)

v(z,0)=w(z,0)=0.

Straightforward computations yield that the vorticity along
x_{1}
-direction is

\partial_{x_{2}}u_{3}-\partial_{x_{3}}u_{2}=\partial_{r}w+\frac{w}{r}.

Therefore, for the flow with large swirl velocity concerned in this paper, its vorticity is
large
in general.
Finally, equation
(
2.7
)
can be rewritten as the Bernoulli’s law:

(2.9)

\partial_{z}\big(r\rho uB\big)+\partial_{r}\big(r\rho vB\big)=0,

where
B:=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(q^{2}+w^{2})+i
is the Bernoulli function.

The eigenvalues of equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) are

\begin{array}[]{lrl}\lambda_{1}&=&\displaystyle\frac{uv}{u^{2}-c^{2}}-\frac{c\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}-c^{2}}}{u^{2}-c^{2}},\\[8.53581pt]
\lambda_{2,3,4}&=&\displaystyle\frac{v}{u},\\[8.53581pt]
\lambda_{5}&=&\displaystyle\frac{uv}{u^{2}-c^{2}}+\frac{c\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}-c^{2}}}{u^{2}-c^{2}}.\end{array}

Let
c^{2}=\displaystyle\frac{\gamma p}{\rho}
be the sonic speed.
M=\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}}}{c}
is called
“quasi-Mach number”
in this paper.
When
M>1
,
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{5}
are real, so the equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) are hyperbolic. We call the flow
“quasi-supersonic flow”
. When
M<1
,
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{5}
are a pair of conjugate complex numbers, so the equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) are of elliptic-hyperbolic composited. We call the flow
“quasi-subsonic flow”
.

Let

\Gamma_{s}:=\left\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:z=\varphi(r),\,0\leq r<r_{0}\right\}

be the shock front position, then the following Rankine-Hugoniot (R.-H.) conditions hold across
\Gamma_{s}
:

(2.10)

\displaystyle[\rho u]-\varphi^{\prime}[\rho v]=0,

(2.11)

\displaystyle[p+\rho u^{2}]-\varphi^{\prime}[\rho uv]=0,

(2.12)

\displaystyle[\rho uv]-\varphi^{\prime}[p+\rho v^{2}]=0,

(2.13)

\displaystyle[\rho uw]-\varphi^{\prime}[\rho vw]=0,

(2.14)

\displaystyle[\frac{1}{2}(q^{2}+w^{2})+i]=0,

where
[\cdot]
denotes the jump of concerned quantities across the shock front
\Gamma_{s}
. Moreover, it follows from (
2.10
), (
2.13
) and (
2.14
) that

(2.15)

\displaystyle[w]=[\frac{1}{2}q^{2}+i]=0.

Denote the states of the flow by

U:=(p,\theta,w,q,s)^{\top},

where
\theta=\arctan\displaystyle\frac{v}{u}
is the flow angle and
q=\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}}
. Then in light of (
1.4
) and (
2.8
) for reasonable boundary conditions, we will study the following free boundary condition governed by 3-d steady axisymmetric Euler equations with a transonic shock.

\llbracket\textit{FBP}\rrbracket
:
Given a supersonic state
U_{-}(r)
at the entrance
\Gamma_{en}
, and a relatively high pressure
p_{+}(r)
at the exit
\Gamma_{ex}
, to find a transonic shock solution
\left(U_{-}(z,r);\ U_{+}(z,r);\ \varphi(r)\right)
such that

(i).

The curve
\Gamma_{s}=\left\{z=\varphi(r):0\leq r<r_{0}\right\}
is the location of the shock front, which separates
\Omega
into two subdomains:

(2.16)

\displaystyle\Omega_{-}=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0<z<\varphi(r),\,0\leq r<r_{0}\},

(2.17)

\displaystyle\Omega_{+}=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\varphi(r)<z<L,\,0\leq r<r_{0}\},

where
\Omega_{-}
denotes the region of the
quasi-supersonic
flow ahead of the shock front, and
\Omega_{+}
is the region of the
quasi-subsonic
flow behind it.

(ii).

U_{-}(z,r)
satisfies (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) in
\Omega_{-}
, and the following boundary conditions:

(2.18)

\displaystyle U_{-}(0,r)=U_{-}(r)

on

\displaystyle\Gamma_{en},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\theta_{-}(z,r_{0})=0

on

\displaystyle\Gamma_{w}\cap\overline{\Omega_{-}},

(2.20)

\displaystyle\theta_{-}(z,0)=w_{-}(z,0)=0

on

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}\cap\overline{\Omega_{-}}.

(iii).

U_{+}(z,r)
satisfies (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) in
\Omega_{+}
, and the following boundary conditions:

(2.21)

\displaystyle p_{+}(L,r)=p_{+}(r)

on

\displaystyle\Gamma_{ex},

(2.22)

\displaystyle\theta_{+}(z,r_{0})=0

on

\displaystyle\Gamma_{w}\cap\overline{\Omega_{+}},

(2.23)

\displaystyle\theta_{+}(z,0)=w_{+}(z,0)=0

on

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}\cap\overline{\Omega_{+}}.

(iv).

\big(U_{-}(z,r);\ U_{+}(z,r);\ \varphi(r)\big)
satisfies the R.-H. conditions (
2.10
)-(
2.14
) across the shock front
\Gamma_{s}
.

Remark 2.1
.

In the definition of
\llbracket\textit{FBP}\rrbracket
, we say the shock solution is transonic when the flow is
quasi-supersonic
ahead of the shock front and
quasi-subsonic
behind of the shock front, instead of the real supersonic and subsonic flow with respect to real Mach number
\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}+w^{2}}}{c}
, because the type of the equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) depends on whether the quasi-Mach number is larger than
1
or not and then the shock solutions in this definition are similar as the transonic shock solutions in the two-dimensional steady Euler flows.

This paper is devoted to establishing the existence of shock solutions

\big(U_{-}(z,r)
,
U_{+}(z,r)
;
\varphi(r)\big)
to the problem
\llbracket\textit{FBP}\rrbracket
, which are a perturbation of special shock solutions with large swirl velocity, and determining their shock locations by carrying out the following steps, which are quickly mentioned in section 1 too.

2.2.
Step 1: Existence of special shock solutions with large non-zero swirl

As previously described, the first step
is to find special shock solutions only depending on
r
.
Namely, we are going to construct shock solutions
\big(\overline{U}_{-}(r);\ \overline{U}_{+}(r);\ \overline{\varphi}(r)\big)
, where the location of the shock front
\overline{\varphi}(r)\equiv\bar{z}_{s}
with
\bar{z}_{s}\in(0,L)
being an arbitrary constant, and

(2.24)

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{-}(r):=\big(\bar{p}_{-}(r),\,0,\,\bar{w}_{-}(r),\,\bar{q}_{-}(r),\,\bar{s}_{-}(r)\big)^{\top},

(2.25)

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{+}(r):=\big(\bar{p}_{+}(r),\,0,\,\bar{w}_{+}(r),\,\bar{q}_{+}(r),\,\bar{s}_{+}(r)\big)^{\top}.

It is easy to check that
\big(\overline{U}_{-}(r);\ \overline{U}_{+}(r)\big)
is a solution of the Euler equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) if and only if
\big(\overline{U}_{-}(r);\ \overline{U}_{+}(r)\big)
satisfies the following ordinary differential equations

(2.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-}(r)=\frac{1}{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(r),

(2.27)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{+}(r)=\frac{1}{r}\bar{\rho}_{+}(r)\bar{w}_{+}^{2}(r).

Moreover, since the shock front is a straight line (
\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}(r)\equiv 0
), the R.-H. conditions
(
2.10
)
-
(
2.14
)
yield that

(2.28)

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{+}=\Big((1+{\mu}^{2})\overline{M}_{-}^{2}-{\mu}^{2}\Big)\bar{p}_{-},

(2.29)

\displaystyle\bar{q}_{+}={\mu}^{2}\Big(\bar{q}_{-}+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{\bar{c}_{-}^{2}}{\bar{q}_{-}}\Big),

(2.30)

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{+}=\frac{{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}}}{\bar{q}_{+}}=\frac{{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}}{\bar{c}_{*}^{2}},

(2.31)

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{+}=\bar{w}_{-},

where

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{-}^{2}=\displaystyle\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}},\quad\overline{M}_{-}^{2}=\displaystyle\frac{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}{\bar{c}_{-}^{2}}=\frac{q_{-}^{2}p_{-}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}-1}}{\gamma A^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}e^{\frac{s_{-}}{\gamma c_{v}}}},\quad\bar{c}_{*}^{2}={\mu}^{2}\Big(\bar{q}_{-}^{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\bar{c}_{-}^{2}\Big),\quad{\mu}^{2}=\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma+1}.

We will prove the following theorem in section 3.

Theorem 2.2
.

Either for given
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{w}_{-}(r),\bar{q}_{-}(r))
with
\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
and
\bar{q}_{-}(r)>0
and for given boundary values
\overline{M}_{-}(0)
and
\bar{p}_{-}(0)
on the symmetry axis with
\overline{M}_{-}(0)>1
and
\bar{p}_{-}(0)>0
, or for given
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{p}_{-}(r),\bar{s}_{-}(r))
with
\bar{p}_{-}(0)>0
and for given boundary value
\bar{q}_{-}(0)>0
on the symmetry axis with
\overline{M}_{-}(0)>1
,
there exists a unique special piecewise
C^{7}
-shock solution
\big(\overline{U}_{-}(r);\ \overline{U}_{+}(r);\ \overline{\varphi}(r)\big)
of the forms (
2.24
) and (
2.25
) to equations (
2.26
)-(
2.27
) in domain
\Omega
with R.-H. conditions (
2.28
)-(
2.31
) up to an arbitrary shift of the shock front location, that is,
z=\overline{\varphi}(r)\equiv\bar{z}_{s}
is the location of the shock front for an arbitrary constant
\bar{z}_{s}\in(0,L)
.

Remark 2.3
.

It is easy to see that solutions
\big(\overline{U}_{-}(r);\ \overline{U}_{+}(r)\big)
of equations (
2.26
)-(
2.27
) with
\theta_{\pm}=0
are solutions of the full Euler system (
2.3
)-(
2.7
).

Remark 2.4
.

In order to establish the existence of quasi-supersonic solutions in Theorem
2.9
, the
C^{7}
-regularity of background solutions is required.

It should be pointed out that for the state
\overline{U}_{-}(r)
ahead of the shock front satisfying the equation (
2.26
), one can obtain the state
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
behind the shock front by the R.-H. conditions (
2.28
)-(
2.31
). But such state
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
does not necessarily satisfies the equation (
2.27
). Therefore, we need to derive an equation for a quantity which is equivalent to equation (
2.27
) if (
2.26
) and (
2.28
)-(
2.31
) hold.
Fortunately, we find that
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)}
plays a key role
in finding the special shock solution, as showed in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

Assume (
2.26
) and (
2.28
)-(
2.31
) hold, then for given
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{w}_{-}(r),\bar{q}_{-}(r))
,
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
satisfies the equation (
2.27
) if and only if
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)}
satisfies the following equation

(2.32)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)}\Big)=\frac{\gamma-1}{4\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(r)}{r}\Big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+\frac{2}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)}}-\big(\gamma-1+\frac{2}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)}\big)\Big).

Thus, for given
C^{7}
-functions
\bar{w}_{-}(r)
and
\bar{q}_{-}(r)
, (
2.32
) is a closed system for
\overline{M}_{-}(r)
. Combining with (
2.26
) and (
2.28
)-(
2.31
), one can determine
C^{7}
-states
\bar{p}_{-}(r)
,
\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)
and
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
. It is the following lemma.

Lemma 2.6
.

For given
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{w}_{-}(r),\bar{q}_{-}(r))
with
\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
and
\bar{q}_{-}(r)>0
and for given boundary values
\overline{M}_{-}(0)
and
\bar{p}_{-}(0)
on the axis with
\overline{M}_{-}(0)>1
and
\bar{p}_{-}(0)>0
, there exist unique
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{p}_{-}(r),\bar{s}_{-}(r))
in the supersonic domain

\Omega_{-}^{\bar{z}_{s}}:=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0<z<\bar{z}_{s},\,0<r<r_{0}\}
,
such that (
2.26
) and (
2.32
) hold. Moreover,
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
given by
(
2.28
)-(
2.31
) satisfies equation (
2.27
).

An inverse version of Lemma
2.6
can also be established by showing the following two lemmas.

Lemma 2.7
.

If (
2.26
) and (
2.28
)-(
2.31
) hold, then for given
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{p}_{-}(r),\bar{s}_{-}(r))
,
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
satisfies the equation (
2.27
) if and only if
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\overline{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}
satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\Big)=

\displaystyle\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{4}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}}{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}\Big\{-\Big(1+\frac{2\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{(\gamma-1)\bar{\rho}_{-}}\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\Big)+\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{(\gamma-1)^{2}\big(1+\frac{2\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{(\gamma-1)\bar{\rho}_{-}}\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\big)}

(2.33)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{4}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\frac{r}{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}}\Big)\Big\}\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}.

For given
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{p}_{-}(r),\bar{s}_{-}(r))
, by (
2.26
), one can obtain
\bar{w}_{-}(r)
. Then (
2.33
) is a closed system for
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\overline{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}
. Therefore one can obtain
\overline{U}_{-}(r)
and then
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
by showing the following lemma.

Lemma 2.8
.

For given
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{p}_{-}(r),\bar{s}_{-}(r))
with
\bar{p}_{-}(0)>0
and for given boundary value
\bar{q}_{-}(0)>0
on the axis with
\overline{M}_{-}(0)>1
, there exist unique
C^{7}
-functions
(\bar{w}_{-}(r),\bar{q}_{-}(r))
in
\Omega_{-}^{\bar{z}_{s}}

such that (
2.26
) and (
2.33
) hold. Moreover,
\overline{U}_{+}(r)
given by (
2.28
)-(
2.31
) satisfies equation (
2.27
).

The proof of Lemmas
2.5
-
2.8
will be given in section 3. Moreover, Theorem
2.2
follows easily from Lemmas
2.5
-
2.8
.

2.3.
Step 2: Existence of small perturbed shock solutions with prescribed receiver pressure.

Based on the special shock solutions established in
Step 1
, the following steps are to study the problem
\llbracket\textit{FBP}\rrbracket
by perturbing the special shock solutions.
More precisely, in problem
\llbracket\textit{FBP}\rrbracket
, we take
U_{-}(r)
in (
2.18
) and
p_{+}(r)
in (
2.21
) as

(2.34)

\displaystyle U_{-}(r):=

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{-}(r)+\sigma U_{en}(r),

(2.35)

\displaystyle p_{+}(r):=

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{+}(r)+\sigma p_{ex}(r),

where
\sigma>0
is a constant and
U_{en}(r):=(p_{en}(r),0,w_{en}(r),q_{en}(r),s_{en}(r))^{\top}
. In addition, we require that for
r\in(0,r_{0})

(2.36)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\bar{p}_{-}(r)+\sigma p_{en}(r)\Big)\neq\frac{1}{r}\Big(\frac{\bar{p}_{-}(r)+\sigma p_{en}(r)}{A}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}e^{-\frac{\bar{s}_{-}(r)+\sigma s_{en}(r)}{\gamma c_{v}}}\big(\bar{w}_{-}(r)+\sigma w_{en}(r)\big)^{2},

which means that
\overline{U}_{-}(r)+\sigma U_{en}(r)
is not a special supersonic solution because it does not satisfy (
2.26
).

By Lemma
2.5
- Lemma
2.8
, we see that for given
(\bar{p}_{-}(r),\bar{s}_{-}(r))
, there exists a unique
(\bar{w}_{-}(r),\bar{q}_{-}(r))
, and vice versa.
So if
(p_{en}(r),s_{en}(r))\neq(0,0)
, by Lemma
2.8
, one can obtain a unique
\big(w_{-}(0,r),q_{-}(0,r)\big)
that satisfies (
2.3
)-(
2.7
). Based on the explicit formula of the solutions given in the proof of Lemma
2.8
in section 3,
\big(w_{-}(0,r),q_{-}(0,r)\big)
is also a small perturbation of
(\bar{w}_{-}(r),\bar{q}_{-}(r))
when
\sigma
is small.
Therefore, without loss of generality, in this paper, we consider

(2.37)

\displaystyle U_{en}(r)=(0,0,w_{en}(r),q_{en}(r),0)^{\top}.

Then requirement (
2.36
) is equivalent to

(2.38)

w_{en}(r)\neq 0\qquad\mbox{for some }r\in(0,r_{0}).

In addition, we impose the following conditions related to boundary conditions and compatibility conditions

(2.39)

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0,\quad\bar{w}_{-}(r_{0})=0,

(2.40)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{q}_{-}(0)=0,\quad\partial_{r}\bar{q}_{-}(r_{0})=0,

(2.41)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{k}\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0,\,k=2,4,6,\quad\partial_{r}^{j}\bar{q}_{-}(0)=0,\,j=3,5,7,

(2.42)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{k}w_{en}(0)=w_{en}(r_{0})=0,\qquad k=0,2,4,6,

(2.43)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{j}q_{en}(0)=\partial_{r}q_{en}(r_{0})=0,\qquad j=1,3,5,7,

(2.44)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}p_{ex}(0)=\partial_{r}p_{ex}(r_{0})=0.

We will consider the transonic shock solutions of problem
\llbracket\textit{FBP}\rrbracket
with conditions (
2.34
)-(
2.35
) and (
2.37
)-(
2.44
).

2.3.1.
Existence of the quasi-supersonic solution.

In dealing with the free boundary problem, the first step is to establish the unique existence of the quasi-supersonic solution ahead of the shock front.

Theorem 2.9
.

Assume that
(w_{en},q_{en})\in C^{7}([0,r_{0}])^{2}
, (
2.38
)-(
2.43
) hold, and the equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) and boundary conditions (
2.18
)-(
2.20
) satisfy the compatibility conditions up to the seventh order, then there exists a sufficiently small positive constant
\sigma_{L}
depending on
\overline{U}_{-}
and
L
, such that for any
0<\sigma<\sigma_{L}
, there exists a unique solution
U_{-}\in C^{3}(\overline{\Omega})
to the equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) with the initial-boundary value conditions (
2.18
)-(
2.20
). Moreover, the following estimate holds

(2.45)

\displaystyle\|U_{-}-\overline{U}_{-}\|_{C^{3}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq C_{-}\sigma\Big(\|w_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}+\|q_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}\Big),

where the positive constant
C_{-}
only depends on
\overline{U}_{-}
,
L
,
r_{0}
and
\gamma
.

Remark 2.10
.

Due to the boundary conditions on the shock for the quasi-subsonic solution behind the shock and to determine the shock location for approximate solutions later, we need at least
C^{3}
-regularity of solution
U_{-}
in the quasi-supersonic domain. We study the hyperbolic system by energy estimates in dimension five to remove the symmetry axis singularity. So
H^{7}
-regularity of
(w_{en},q_{en})
is required. See section 4 for details of the proof.

2.3.2.
Approximate shock position, and the quasi-subsonic solution behind the free shock front.

One of the main difficulties is to determine the position of the shock front, while the shock position for the special shock solution is arbitrary.
In order to overcome it,
we will introduce a specific free boundary problem by linearizing the Euler system and boundary conditions around the special solutions and ignoring the higher order error terms in section 6.
Most importantly, the shock location can be uniquely determined in the specific free boundary problem via the solvability condition, under certain sufficient conditions.
This solution and its shock front, which we call the approximate shock solution and the approximate shock front, will be served as the first order approximation of the solutions and the shock front of the nonlinear problem for the perturbed data.
Then in section 6, a nonlinear iteration scheme is designed around this approximate shock solution with a higher order error of the perturbation, which leads us to the existence of the perturbed shock solution with the shock front being close to the approximate shock front.
Actually, to determine the approximate shock front position
\dot{z}_{*}
, in section 6.1, we introduce

\displaystyle{I}_{1}({z}):=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r_{0}}r\bar{p}_{+}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(r)(1-\kappa_{1}(r))\frac{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)-1}{\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}\dot{p}_{-}({z},r)\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\int_{0}^{{z}}r\bar{p}_{+}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(r)\kappa_{3}(r)\dot{\theta}_{-}(\tau,r)\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle I_{2}:=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{\overline{M}_{+}^{2}(r)-1}{\bar{\rho}_{+}(r)\bar{q}_{+}^{2}(r)}r\bar{p}_{+}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(r)p_{ex}(r)\mathrm{d}r-\int_{0}^{r_{0}}r\bar{p}_{+}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(r)\kappa_{2}(r)q_{en}(r)\mathrm{d}r.

Here
\dot{p}_{-}
and
\dot{\theta}_{-}
are the solution of the linearized Euler system for quasi-supersonic flow (see (
6.1
)-(
6.5
)), and
\kappa_{i}(r)
for
i=1,2,3
are the quantities only depending on the background solutions
\overline{U}_{\pm}(r)
(see (
6.23
)-(
6.25
)). Then as discussed in section 6.1, the solvability condition (
6.11
) is equivalent to

(2.46)

I_{1}(\dot{z}_{*})=\sigma I_{2}.

Actually,
\dot{z}_{*}\in(0,L)
is determined by the following lemma.

Lemma 2.11
.

Assume that
(w_{en},q_{en})\in C^{7}([0,r_{0}])^{2}
,
p_{ex}(r)\in C^{2,\alpha}([0,r_{0}])
, (
2.38
)-(
2.44
) hold, the equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) and boundary conditions (
2.18
)-(
2.20
) satisfy the compatibility conditions up to the seventh order, and

(2.47)

\displaystyle I_{3}:=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{i_{1}(r)}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}(r)}{r}w_{en}(r)\mathrm{d}r>0,

where

\displaystyle i_{1}(r):=

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{+}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(r)\Big((1-\kappa_{1}(r))\big(\frac{\bar{w}_{+}^{2}(r)}{\bar{c}_{+}^{2}(r)}-\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(r)}{\bar{c}_{-}^{2}(r)}\big)+r\kappa_{3}(r)-r\partial_{r}\kappa_{1}(r)\Big).

Then there exist a positive constant
C_{(\overline{U}_{-})}
depending on
\overline{U}_{-}
, and a constant
L_{*}
satisfying

(2.48)

\displaystyle 0<L_{*}<\frac{4I_{3}}{C_{(\overline{U}_{-})}(\|w_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}+\|q_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})})\int_{0}^{r_{0}}|i_{1}(r)|\mathrm{d}r},

such that if

(2.49)

\displaystyle 0<I_{2}\leq L_{*}^{2}\Big(I_{3}-\frac{C_{(\overline{U}_{-})}(\|w_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}+\|q_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})})}{6}L_{*}\int_{0}^{r_{0}}|i_{1}(r)|\mathrm{d}r\Big),

then there exists a unique
\dot{z}_{*}\in(0,L_{*})
such that (
2.46
) holds.

Remark 2.12
.

It is easy to see that condition (
2.47
) fails if
\bar{w}_{-}(r)=0
, that is the case when small swirl solution is a small perturbation of constant background solutions. Moreover, conditions (
2.48
) and (
2.49
) will be easier to hold if the non-zero swirl
\bar{w}_{-}
of the special shock solution is larger. Therefore, non-zero swirl velocity plays a fundamental role in determining the shock location.

Based on the obtained approximate shock front position
\dot{z}_{*}
, we introduce the following coordinates transformation to fix the free boundary
\Gamma_{s}

\pounds_{\dot{z}_{*}}^{-1}:(z,\,r)=\big(L+\frac{L-\varphi(\tilde{r})}{L-\dot{z}_{*}}(\tilde{z}-L),\,\tilde{r}\big).

In the new coordinates
(\tilde{z},\tilde{r})
, shock front
\Gamma_{s}
becomes
\dot{\Gamma}_{s}=\{(\dot{z}_{*},\tilde{r});\,\tilde{r}\in[0,r_{0}]\}
, which is fixed.
We define the norms of the function
u
as below:

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Gamma_{s})}:=

\displaystyle\|u\circ\pounds_{\dot{z}_{*}}^{-1}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\dot{\Gamma}_{s})},

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{k,\alpha}({\Omega}_{+})}:=

\displaystyle\|u\circ\pounds_{\dot{z}_{*}}^{-1}\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(\dot{\Omega}_{+})},\,k=1,2.

Then we will show the main theorem of this paper.

Theorem 2.13
.

Under the assumptions given in Lemma
2.11
, there exist a positive constant
r_{*}
and a sufficiently small constant
\sigma_{0}>0
, only depending on
\overline{U}_{-}(r)
and
\gamma
, such that for
0<r_{0}\leq r_{*}
and
0<\sigma\leq\sigma_{0}
, there exists a shock solution
(U_{-},U_{+};\ \varphi(r))
to the free boundary value problem
\llbracket\textit{FBP}\rrbracket
. Moreover,

(2.50)

\displaystyle\|U_{-}-\overline{U}_{-}\|_{C^{3}(\overline{\Omega}_{-})}\leq C_{-}\sigma\Big(\|w_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}+\|q_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}\Big),

\displaystyle\|p_{+}-\bar{p}_{+}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}({\Omega}_{+})}+\|\theta_{+}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}({\Omega}_{+})}+\|w_{+}-\bar{w}_{+}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}({\Omega}_{+})}+\|q_{+}-\bar{q}_{+}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}({\Omega}_{+})}

\displaystyle+\|s_{+}-\bar{s}_{+}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}({\Omega}_{+})}+\|rw_{+}-r\bar{w}_{+}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}({\Omega}_{+})}+\|\varphi^{\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Gamma_{s})}

(2.51)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{+}\sigma\Big(\|w_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}+\|q_{en}\|_{H^{7}(\Gamma_{en})}+\|p_{ex}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Gamma_{ex})}\Big),

where the positive constant
C_{-}
depends on
\overline{U}_{-}
,
L
,
r_{0}
and
\gamma
, and the positive constant
C_{+}
depends on
\overline{U}_{\pm}
,
L
,
r_{0}
,
\gamma
and
\alpha
. In addition, the shock location satisfies

(2.52)

\displaystyle|\varphi(r_{0})-\dot{z}_{*}|\leq C\sigma,

where the positive constant
C
depends on
\overline{U}_{\pm}
,
L
,
r_{0}
,
\gamma
and
\alpha
.

3.
Special steady normal shock solutions with non-zero swirl

In this section, we first prove Lemma
2.5
- Lemma
2.8
. Then we will state properties of such special shock solutions.

3.1.
Existence of steady special shock solutions

In this subsection, we will prove Lemma
2.5
- Lemma
2.8
one by one, and then Theorem
2.2
follows.

Proof of Lemma
2.5
.

By (
2.28
)-(
2.30
), we have

(3.1)

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{+}=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}-\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma+1}\Big)\bar{p}_{-}=\frac{2}{\gamma+1}\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}-\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma+1}\bar{p}_{-},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{+}=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}}{{\mu}^{2}\Big(\bar{q}_{-}+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{\bar{c}_{-}^{2}}{\bar{q}_{-}}\Big)}=\frac{(\gamma+1)\bar{\rho}_{-}}{(\gamma-1)\Big(1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)}.

By applying (
3.1
), (
3.2
) and (
2.31
), it follows from (
2.27
) that

(3.3)

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\gamma+1}\partial_{r}(\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2})-\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma+1}\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-}=\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\frac{(\gamma+1)\bar{\rho}_{-}}{(\gamma-1)\Big(1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)}.

Substituting (
2.26
) into (
3.3
), one has

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma+1}{2}\Big(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\frac{(\gamma+1)\bar{\rho}_{-}}{(\gamma-1)\big(1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\big)}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma+1}\frac{1}{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{w}_{-}^{2}\Big)

(3.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{2r}\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}^{2}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}\Big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{2+(\gamma-1)\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big).

Applying (
3.4
) and (
2.26
), one has

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)=

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\Big)=\frac{\gamma}{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-}-\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}^{2}\bar{q}_{-}^{4}}\partial_{r}(\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\frac{1}{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{w}_{-}^{2}-\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}^{2}\bar{q}_{-}^{4}}\frac{1}{2r}\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}^{2}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}\bar{w}_{-}^{2}\Big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{2+(\gamma-1)\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)

(3.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\Big(\gamma-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{2+(\gamma-1)\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)\Big).

Further calculations yield (
2.32
) easily.

On the other hand, if (
2.32
) holds, by reversing the argument from (
2.32
) to (
3.5
), we know that (
3.5
) holds. Then applying (
2.31
), (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), one has

(3.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{+}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\gamma+1}\partial_{r}(\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2})-\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma+1}\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\bar{\rho}_{+}\bar{w}_{+}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\frac{(\gamma+1)\bar{\rho}_{-}}{(\gamma-1)\Big(1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)}.

By (
3.5
), one has

(3.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2})=\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}{\bar{p}_{-}}\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-}-\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}^{2}\bar{q}_{-}^{4}}{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big).

Then applying (
2.26
), (
2.32
), (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), it is easy to check that (
2.27
) holds.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Let
t(r):=\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)}
, then (
2.32
) becomes

(3.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{t}{\gamma-1}\Big)=\frac{\gamma-1}{4\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\Big(-\big(1+\frac{2t}{\gamma-1}\big)+\frac{1}{\mu^{4}}\frac{1}{1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}t}\Big).

Let

(3.10)

\displaystyle T(r):=1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}t(r).

Then (
3.9
) yields that

(3.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}T=(\gamma-1)\frac{1}{r}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{2\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\Big(-T+\frac{1}{\mu^{4}T}\Big)=(\gamma-1)\frac{1}{r}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{2\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\frac{1-\mu^{4}T^{2}}{\mu^{4}T},

which implies that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\ln(1-\mu^{4}T^{2})\Big)=-(\gamma-1)\frac{1}{r}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}.

Therefore, there exist a unique
T(r)
of the form:

(3.13)

\displaystyle T(r)=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\sqrt{1-\big(1-\mu^{4}T^{2}(0)\big)e^{-(\gamma-1)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{1}{\tau}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}\mathrm{d}\tau}},

where
T(0)=1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(0)}\in(1,\frac{\gamma+1}{\gamma-1})
. It follows from (
3.10
) that

(3.14)

\displaystyle t(r)=\frac{\gamma+1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{1-\big(1-\mu^{4}T^{2}(0)\big)e^{-(\gamma-1)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{1}{\tau}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}\mathrm{d}\tau}}-\mu^{2}\Big).

Moreover, the existence is global for any
r>0
, because it follows from
t(0)=\frac{1}{\bar{M}_{-}^{2}(0)}\in(0,1)
that

\displaystyle 1-\mu^{4}T^{2}(0)=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{(\gamma+1)^{2}}\Big(1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}t(0)\Big)^{2}

(3.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{(\gamma-1+2t(0))^{2}}{(\gamma+1)^{2}}\in(0,1).

In addition, (
3.14
) implies that for any
r\in(0,+\infty)
,
t(r)
is monotonely increasing with respect to
r
. Hence, we have for any
r\in(0,+\infty)

(3.16)

\displaystyle 0<t(0)\leq t(r)<\frac{\gamma+1}{2}(1-\mu^{2})=1.

Once
t(r)
is obtained, then employing (
2.26
), one has

(3.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-}(r)=\frac{1}{r}\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}(r)}{t(r)\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(r).

Then

(3.18)

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{-}(r)=\bar{p}_{-}(0)\exp\Big(\gamma\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}{\tau t(\tau)\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}\mathrm{d}\tau\Big),

where we use the conditions that
\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
and
\bar{w}_{-}
is smooth.
Then

(3.19)

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)=\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}(r)}{t(r)\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}=\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}(0)}{t(r)\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}\exp\Big(\gamma\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}{\tau t(\tau)\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}\mathrm{d}\tau\Big).

By
\bar{p}_{-}=Ae^{\frac{\bar{s}_{-}}{c_{v}}}\bar{\rho}_{-}^{\gamma}
, we have
\bar{s}_{-}={c_{v}}\ln\big(\frac{\bar{p}_{-}}{A\bar{\rho}_{-}^{\gamma}}\big)
.

Finally,
C^{7}
-regularity of the solutions follows easily from the solution expressions.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.7
.

Based on Lemma
2.5
, it is sufficient to show that (
2.32
) is equivalent to (
2.33
). By a similar argument as done in the proof of Lemma
2.5
, we can see that both (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) are equivalent to the following equation

(3.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}}\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\Big)=\frac{\gamma-1}{4}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\Big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+\frac{2\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}}-\big(\gamma-1+\frac{2\gamma\bar{p}_{-}}{\bar{\rho}_{-}\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\big)\Big)\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}.

Since the computation is straightward and long, we omit it for the shortness.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.8
.

Since quantities
\bar{p}_{-}(r)
and
\bar{s}_{-}(r)
are given, by the state equation
\bar{p}_{-}(r)=Ae^{\frac{\bar{s}(r)}{c_{v}}}\bar{\rho}_{-}^{\gamma}
, one has

(3.21)

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)=\Big(\frac{\bar{p}_{-}(r)}{A}e^{-\frac{\bar{s}_{-}(r)}{c_{v}}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}.

Then it follows from (
2.26
) that

(3.22)

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(r)=\frac{r\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-}(r)}{\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)}=\frac{r\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{-}(r)}{\Big(\frac{\bar{p}_{-}(r)}{A}e^{-\frac{\bar{s}_{-}(r)}{c_{v}}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}.

Let
Q(r):=\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)}
and
G(r):=\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)}{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}(r)}
, then (
2.33
) yields that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(\frac{2Q}{(\gamma-1)G}\right)=\frac{(\gamma-1)\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{2r}\Big\{-\Big(1+\frac{2Q}{(\gamma-1)G}\Big)+\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{(\gamma-1)^{2}\big(1+\frac{2Q}{(\gamma-1)G}\big)}\Big\}.

Let

Y(r):=1+\frac{2Q(r)}{(\gamma-1)G(r)}.

Then

(3.24)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}Y=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\frac{1-\mu^{4}Y^{2}}{\mu^{4}Y}.

Direct calculations yield that

(3.25)

\displaystyle Y(r)=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\sqrt{1-\big(1-\mu^{4}Y^{2}(0)\big)e^{-(\gamma-1)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}{\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau}},

where

(3.26)

\displaystyle Y(0)=1+\frac{2Q(0)}{(\gamma-1)G(0)}=1+\frac{2\gamma A^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(\bar{p}_{-}(0))^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}}e^{\frac{\bar{s}_{-}(0)}{\gamma c_{v}}}}{(\gamma-1)\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(0)}\in\Big(1,\frac{\gamma+1}{\gamma-1}\Big).

Hence
Y(r)
exists uniquely and globally. In addition,
Y(r)
is monotonically increasing with respect to
r
such that

1<Y(0)\leq Y(r)<\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}=\frac{\gamma+1}{\gamma-1}.

Thus,

\displaystyle Q(r)=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma-1}{2}(Y(r)-1)G(r)

(3.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma+1}{2}\frac{\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)}{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}(r)}\Big(\sqrt{1-\big(1-\mu^{4}Y^{2}(0)\big)e^{-(\gamma-1)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(\tau)}{\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau}}-\mu^{2}\Big).

By (
3.21
) and (
3.22
), we can obtain
\bar{q}_{-}^{2}(r)=\frac{1}{Q(r)}
.

Finally, the
C^{7}
-regularity of solutions follows easily from the solution expressions.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

The existence of the special shock solutions is obtained by Lemma
2.6
and Lemma
2.8
. Note that the special solutions do not depend on
z
, so the shock location
\bar{\varphi}=\bar{z}_{s}
can be arbitrary.
∎

3.2.
Properties of the special shock solutions

The special transonic shock solution
(\overline{U}_{-}(r),\overline{U}_{+}(r))
obtained in Theorem
2.2
actually satisfies the following properties.

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume that (
2.39
) and (
2.40
) hold, the special transonic shock solution
(\overline{U}_{-}(r),\overline{U}_{+}(r))
obtained in Theorem
2.2
satisfies

(3.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\bar{p}_{\pm})(0)=\partial_{r}(\bar{s}_{\pm})(0)=\partial_{r}(\overline{M}_{\pm}^{2})(0)=0,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\bar{p}_{\pm})(r_{0})=\partial_{r}(\bar{s}_{\pm})(r_{0})=\partial_{r}(\overline{M}_{\pm}^{2})(r_{0})=0.

Moreover, if (
2.41
) holds, then

(3.30)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{j}(\bar{p}_{\pm},\bar{\rho}_{\pm},\bar{s}_{\pm})(0)=0,\quad\partial_{r}^{j}\bar{q}_{+}(0)=0,\quad\partial_{r}^{j}({\overline{M}_{\pm}^{2}})(0)=0\qquad\mbox{where }j=3,5,7.

Proof.

Applying (
2.31
) and (
2.39
), we have
\bar{w}_{\pm}(0)=\bar{w}_{\pm}(r_{0})=0
.
Then it follows from (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) that

(3.31)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{\pm}(0)=\partial_{r}\bar{p}_{\pm}(r_{0})=0.

At the same time, it follows from (
2.32
) and the smoothness of
\bar{w}_{-}
that

(3.32)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)(0)=\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)(r_{0})=0.

So

(3.33)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(0)=\partial_{r}\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r_{0})=0.

By (
2.29
),

(3.34)

\displaystyle\bar{q}_{+}=\mu^{2}\bar{q}_{-}\Big(1+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big).

So it follows from (
2.40
), (
3.32
) and (
3.34
) that

(3.35)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{q}_{+}(0)=\partial_{r}\bar{q}_{+}(r_{0})=0.

Moreover, by (
2.40
), (
2.39
) and (
3.4
), we have

(3.36)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}(0)=\partial_{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}(r_{0})=0.

Then by (
3.2
)

(3.37)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bar{\rho}_{+}(0)=\partial_{r}\bar{\rho}_{+}(r_{0})=0.

So it follows from (
3.31
), (
3.35
) and (
3.37
) that

(3.38)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{+}^{2}}\Big)(0)=\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{+}^{2}}\Big)(r_{0})=0.

So

(3.39)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\overline{M}_{+}^{2}(0)=\partial_{r}\overline{M}_{+}^{2}(r_{0})=0.

Finally, by the thermal relation
p=Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}\rho^{\gamma}
, we have

(3.40)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}s=c_{v}\frac{\partial_{r}p}{p}-\gamma c_{v}\frac{\partial_{r}\rho}{\rho}.

Therefore,
\partial_{r}\bar{s}_{\pm}(0)=\partial_{r}\bar{s}_{\pm}(r_{0})=0
.

Now, we are going to prove (
3.30
) for
j=3
.
By applying
\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
and
\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
, we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}\partial_{r}^{2}(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r})=

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}2\big(\frac{(\partial_{r}\bar{w}_{-})^{2}+\bar{w}_{-}\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{w}_{-}}{r}-\frac{2\bar{w}_{-}\partial_{r}\bar{w}_{-}}{r^{2}}+\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r^{3}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\partial_{r}\bar{w}_{-}(0)\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{w}_{-}(0)+\frac{2}{3}\bar{w}_{-}(0)\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{w}_{-}(0)

(3.41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

By (
2.26
), direct calculations yield that

(3.42)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{p}_{-}(r)=\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)+2\partial_{r}(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r})\partial_{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}+\bar{\rho}_{-}\partial_{r}^{2}(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}).

Therefore,

(3.43)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{p}_{-}(0)=0.

Similarly, by (
2.27
) and
\bar{w}_{+}(0)=\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
,
\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{w}_{+}(0)=\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
and (
3.37
), we have

(3.44)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{p}_{+}(0)=0.

Recalling the definition
t(r)=\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(r)}
and by (
2.32
), we have

(3.45)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}t=\frac{\gamma-1}{4\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\Big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+2t}-\big(\gamma-1+2t\big)\Big).

So

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}t=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma-1}{4}\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\Big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+2t}-\big(\gamma-1+2t\big)\Big)\partial_{r}^{2}(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r})

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma-1}{4}\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+2t}-\big(\gamma-1+2t\big)\big)\Big)\partial_{r}(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r})

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma-1}{4}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+2t}-\big(\gamma-1+2t\big)\big)\Big)

(3.46)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma-1}{4}\partial_{r}(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r})\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{1}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+2t}-\big(\gamma-1+2t\big)\big)\Big).

It follows from
\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
,
\partial_{r}\bar{q}_{-}(0)=0
, (
3.32
), (
3.41
), and (
3.46
) that
\partial_{r}^{3}t(0)=0
. That is

(3.47)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)(0)=0.

Hence

(3.48)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}(0)=0.

By
\bar{\rho}_{-}=\frac{\gamma\bar{p}_{-}\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}{\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}
, it follows from (
3.28
), (
2.41
), (
3.43
), (
3.48
), and (
2.41
) that

(3.49)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{\rho}_{-}(0)=0.

By (
3.28
), (
3.40
), (
3.43
), and (
3.49
), we have

(3.50)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{s}_{-}(0)=0.

By (
2.29
), further calculations yield that

(3.51)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{q}_{+}=\mu^{2}\Big((1+\frac{2t}{\gamma-1})\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{q}_{-}+\frac{2\bar{q}_{-}}{\gamma-1}\partial_{r}^{3}t+\frac{6}{\gamma-1}\partial_{r}(\partial_{r}\bar{q}_{-}\partial_{r}t)\Big).

So it follows from (
3.28
), (
2.41
), (
3.47
), and (
3.51
) that

(3.52)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{q}_{+}(0)=0.

Similarly, we have

(3.53)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{\rho}_{+}(0)=\partial_{r}^{3}\overline{M}_{+}^{2}(0)=\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{s}_{+}(0)=0.

Taking forth derivatives on the both sides of (
2.26
) with respect to the variable
r
, we have

(3.54)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}\bar{p}_{-}(r)=\partial_{r}^{4}\big(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(r)}{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}(r)\big).

By
\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
in (
2.39
), (
3.41
), (
3.49
), and
\partial_{r}\bar{\rho}_{-}(0)=0
in (
3.36
), it follows from (
3.54
) that

(3.55)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}\bar{p}_{-}(r)(0)=\bar{\rho}_{-}(0)\partial_{r}^{4}\big(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(r)}{r}\big).

By
\partial_{r}^{k}\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
,
k=0,2,4
in (
2.41
), we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}\partial_{r}^{4}(\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\partial_{r}^{5}\bar{w}_{-}^{2}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\big(\bar{w}_{-}(0)\partial_{r}^{5}\bar{w}_{-}(0)+5\partial_{r}\bar{w}_{-}(0)\partial_{r}^{4}\bar{w}_{-}(0)+10\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{w}_{-}(0)\partial_{r}^{3}\bar{w}_{-}(0)\big)

(3.56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

So

(3.57)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}\bar{p}_{-}(0)=0.

Then by
\bar{w}_{+}(r)=\bar{w}_{-}(r)
and (
2.27
), a similar calculation yields that

(3.58)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}\bar{p}_{+}(0)=0.

Taking forth derivatives on the both sides of (
2.26
) with respect to the variable
r
, we have

(3.59)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}t=\partial_{r}^{4}\Big(\frac{\gamma-1}{4\bar{q}_{-}^{2}}\frac{\bar{w}_{-}^{2}}{r}\big(\frac{(\gamma+1)^{2}}{\gamma-1+2t}-\big(\gamma-1+2t\big)\big)\Big).

By
\bar{w}_{-}(0)=0
in (
2.39
),
\partial_{r}^{j}\bar{q}_{-}(0)=0
for
j=1,3
in (
2.41
), (
3.38
), (
3.41
), (
3.47
), and (
3.56
), it follows from (
3.59
) that
\partial_{r}^{5}t(0)=0
, that is

(3.60)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{M}_{-}^{2}}\Big)(0)=0.

So it follows from (
3.33
), (
3.48
) and (
3.60
) that

(3.61)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}\overline{M}_{-}^{2}(0)=0.

Following a similar calculation as done for (
3.49
)-(
3.53
), by (
2.41
), (
3.29
), (
3.30
) for
j=3
, (
3.57
), (
3.58
), and (
3.61
), we have

(3.62)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{5}(\bar{p}_{+},\bar{\rho}_{\pm},\bar{s}_{\pm})(0)=0,\quad\partial_{r}^{5}\bar{q}_{+}(0)=0.

Thus, we have proven (
3.30
) for
j=5
.

Similarly, we can prove (
3.30
) for
j=7
from (
2.41
). Because the argument is similar and long, we omit it for shortness.
∎

4.
Quasi-supersonic solutions in the cylinder

In this section, we will consider the existence of quasi-supersonic solutions in the whole domain
\Omega
by proving Theorem
2.9
.
Before doing that, the compatibility conditions up to the seventh order for the initial-boundary value problem (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) and (
2.18
)-(
2.20
) with (
2.37
) and (
2.39
)-(
2.44
) will be given.

To simplify the notations, we will drop the subscript “
-
” to write
U_{-}
as
U
in this section.
First, it is easy to see that the zero order and first order compatibility conditions have already been given in (
2.39
)-(
2.44
).
Then we will derive the higher order compatibility conditions by induction. The equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) can be rewritten in the following matrix form:

(4.1)

\displaystyle A(U)U_{z}+B(U)U_{r}+C(U)=0.

Taking derivatives on the both sides of the equation (
4.1
) with respect to the variables
z
and
r
respectively, we have in
\Omega

(4.2)

\displaystyle A(U)U_{zz}+B(U)U_{zr}+D_{U}A(U)U_{z}U_{z}+D_{U}B(U)U_{z}U_{r}+D_{U}C(U)U_{z}=0,

(4.3)

\displaystyle A(U)U_{zr}+B(U)U_{rr}+D_{U}A(U)U_{r}U_{z}+D_{U}B(U)U_{r}U_{r}+D_{U}C(U)U_{r}=0.

Take twice tangential derivatives on the boundary conditions (
2.18
)-(
2.20
) with (
2.37
), then

(4.4)

\displaystyle U_{rr}=\partial_{rr}\overline{U}_{-}+\sigma\partial_{rr}U_{en}\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\Gamma_{en}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{zz}\theta=0\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\Gamma_{w}\cup\Gamma_{a}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{zz}w=0\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\Gamma_{a}.

Solving equations (
4.2
)-(
4.6
) with equation (
4.3
), boundary conditions (
2.18
)-(
2.20
) and the zeroth and first order compatibility conditions at point
(z,r)=(0,0)
or
(z,r)=(0,r_{0})
, we can obtain the second order compatibility conditions. Then by taking one more derivatives for each time, we can obtain the compatibility conditions up to the seventh order.

Before giving the proof of Theorem
2.9
, we would like to give the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

For any fixed integer
n\geq 2
, and for
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},x_{n+1})
, set
r(\mathbf{x}):=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}}
and
B_{R}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:\,r(\mathbf{x})\leq R\}
.Then for
m=1,2
,
\Phi(\mathbf{x}):=\varphi(r(\mathbf{x}),x_{n+1})\in C^{m,\alpha}(B_{R})
if and only if
\varphi(r,x_{n+1})\in C^{m,\alpha}([0,R]\times\mathbb{R})
and
\varphi_{r}(0,x_{n+1})=0
. For
m=3,4
,
\Phi(\mathbf{x}):=\varphi(r(\mathbf{x}),x_{n+1})\in C^{m,\alpha}(B_{R})
if and only if
\varphi(r,x_{n+1})\in C^{m,\alpha}([0,R]\times\mathbb{R})
and
\varphi_{r}^{j}(0,x_{n+1})=0
for
j=1,3
. In addition, there exists a constant
C_{m}
depending only on
\alpha
and
R
, such that

C_{m}\|\Phi\|_{C^{m,\alpha}(B_{R})}\leq\|\varphi\|_{C^{m,\alpha}([0,R]\times\mathbb{R})}\leq\|\Phi\|_{C^{m,\alpha}(B_{R})}
. Moreover, if
\varphi_{r}^{j}(0,x_{n+1})=0
for
j=1,3,5
, and if
\varphi(r,x_{n+1})\in C^{7}([0,R]\times\mathbb{R})
, then
\Phi(\mathbf{x}):=\varphi(r(\mathbf{x}),x_{n+1})\in H^{7}(B_{R})
and satisfies

\|\Phi\|_{H^{7}(B_{R})}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{H^{7}([0,R]\times\mathbb{R})}
.

Proof.

The case
m=1,2
is from lemma in
[
43
, Lemma 2.1]
, which only considers the two-dimensional case and the proof can be generalized to higher dimensions easily. The proof for case
m=3,4
can be easily done by combining the proof of the Lemma in
[
43
, Lemma 2.1]
and the computation for the case of
H^{7}
, so we omit their proofs for the shortness.

For the case of
H^{7}
, by
[
34
]
, for any given multi-indices
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{n})
with
\beta_{i}\geq 0
for
i=1,\cdots,n,n+1
,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}\partial_{x_{n+1}}^{\beta_{n+1}}\Phi=\sum_{k=0}^{[\frac{|\beta|}{2}]}\frac{1}{2^{k}k!}(\triangle_{\mathbf{x}}^{k}\mathbf{x}^{\beta})\cdot(D^{|\beta|-k}\partial_{x_{n+1}}^{\beta_{n+1}}\varphi)(r,x_{n+1}),

where
|\beta|=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{n}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\beta}=x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}
,
\triangle_{\mathbf{x}}
denotes the Laplace operator and

(4.8)

\displaystyle(D\varphi)(r,x_{n+1}):=\frac{\partial_{r}\varphi(r,x_{n+1})}{r}.

Without loss of generality, let us only consider the case that
|\beta|=7
and
\beta_{3}=\beta_{4}=\cdots=\beta_{n+1}=0
, that is,
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},0,\cdots,0)
and
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=7
.
It follows from (
4.7
) that for
i,j=1,2
and
i\neq j
,

(4.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}^{7}\Phi=

\displaystyle 105x_{i}D^{4}\varphi+105x_{i}^{3}D^{5}\varphi+21x_{i}^{5}D^{6}\varphi+x_{i}^{7}D^{7}\varphi

(4.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{j}}^{6}\Phi=

\displaystyle 15x_{i}D^{4}\varphi+45x_{i}x_{j}^{2}D^{5}\varphi+15x_{i}x_{j}^{4}D^{6}\varphi+x_{i}x_{j}^{6}D^{7}\varphi

(4.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}\partial_{x_{j}}^{5}\Phi=

\displaystyle 15x_{j}D^{4}\varphi+5\big(3x_{i}^{2}x_{j}+2x_{j}^{3}\big)D^{5}\varphi+\big(x_{j}^{5}+10x_{i}^{2}x_{j}^{3}\big)D^{6}\varphi+x_{i}^{2}x_{j}^{5}D^{7}\varphi

(4.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}^{3}\partial_{x_{j}}^{4}\Phi=

\displaystyle 9x_{i}D^{4}\varphi+18x_{i}x_{j}^{2}D^{5}\varphi+3\big(x_{i}x_{j}^{4}+x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{2}\big)D^{6}\varphi+x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{4}D^{7}\varphi.

It follows from (
4.8
) and direct computation that
D\varphi=\frac{\varphi_{r}}{r}
,
D^{2}\varphi=\frac{\varphi_{rr}}{r^{2}}-\frac{\varphi_{r}}{r^{3}}
,
D^{3}\varphi=\frac{\varphi_{rrr}}{r^{3}}-\frac{3\varphi_{rr}}{r^{4}}+\frac{3\varphi_{r}}{r^{5}}
, and

\displaystyle D^{4}\varphi=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{r}^{4}\varphi}{r^{4}}-\frac{6\partial_{r}^{3}\varphi}{r^{5}}+\frac{15\partial_{r}^{2}\varphi}{r^{6}}-\frac{15\varphi_{r}}{r^{7}}

\displaystyle D^{5}\varphi=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{r}^{5}\varphi}{r^{5}}-\frac{10\partial_{r}^{4}\varphi}{r^{6}}+\frac{45\partial_{r}^{3}\varphi}{r^{7}}-\frac{105\partial_{r}^{2}\varphi}{r^{8}}+\frac{105\varphi_{r}}{r^{9}}

\displaystyle D^{6}\varphi=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{r}^{6}\varphi}{r^{6}}-\frac{15\partial_{r}^{5}\varphi}{r^{7}}+\frac{105\partial_{r}^{4}\varphi}{r^{8}}-\frac{420\partial_{r}^{3}\varphi}{r^{9}}+\frac{945\partial_{r}^{2}\varphi}{r^{10}}-\frac{945\varphi_{r}}{r^{11}}

\displaystyle D^{7}\varphi=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{r}^{7}\varphi}{r^{7}}-\frac{21\partial_{r}^{6}\varphi}{r^{8}}+\frac{210\partial_{r}^{5}\varphi}{r^{9}}-\frac{1260\partial_{r}^{4}\varphi}{r^{10}}+\frac{4725\partial_{r}^{3}\varphi}{r^{11}}-\frac{10395\partial_{r}^{2}\varphi}{r^{12}}+\frac{10395\varphi_{r}}{r^{13}}

Note that
\varphi\in C^{7}
and
\varphi_{r}(0)=\partial_{r}^{3}\varphi(0)=\partial_{r}^{5}\varphi(0)=0
, by L’H
\hat{o}
pital’s rule,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{7}D^{7}\varphi=-\frac{5}{16}\partial_{r}^{7}\varphi(0),\quad\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{5}D^{6}\varphi=\frac{1}{16}\partial_{r}^{7}\varphi(0),

(4.14)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{3}D^{5}\varphi=-\frac{1}{48}\partial_{r}^{7}\varphi(0),\quad\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}rD^{4}\varphi=\frac{1}{48}\partial_{r}^{7}\varphi(0).

So we know for
|\beta|=7
,
\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}\Phi
are bounded functions.
Therefore, if
\varphi(r,x_{n+1})\in C^{7}([0,R]\times\mathbb{R})
and
\partial_{r}^{k}\varphi(0,x_{n+1})=0,k=1,3,5
, then
\Phi(\mathbf{x}):=\varphi(r(\mathbf{x}),x_{n+1})\in H^{7}(B_{R})
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
2.9
.

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

If the solutions are smooth, equations (
2.3
)-(
2.7
) can be rewritten as follows.

(4.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}(r\rho u)+\partial_{r}(r\rho v)=0,

(4.16)

\displaystyle uu_{z}+vu_{r}+\frac{1}{\rho}p_{z}=0,

(4.17)

\displaystyle uw_{z}+vw_{r}+\frac{vw}{r}=0,

(4.18)

\displaystyle uB_{z}+vB_{r}=0,

(4.19)

\displaystyle us_{z}+vs_{r}=0.

It follows from (
4.15
) that there exists a function
\psi
such that

\displaystyle\nabla\psi=(\psi_{z},\psi_{r})=(-r\rho v,r\rho u),

so

(4.20)

\displaystyle v=-\frac{\psi_{z}}{r\rho},\quad u=\frac{\psi_{r}}{r\rho}.

Then for any
r\in(0,r_{0})
, one has

(4.21)

\displaystyle\psi(0,r)=\int_{0}^{r}\tau\rho(0,\tau)u(0,\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau,

where it follows from (
2.34
) and (
2.37
) that

(4.22)

(p(0,r),\theta(0,r),w(0,r),q(0,r),s(0,r))=(\bar{p}_{-},0,\bar{w}_{-}+\sigma w_{en},\bar{q}_{-}+\sigma q_{en},\bar{s}_{-})

and then

(4.23)

\rho(0,r)=\left(\frac{p(0,r)}{Ae^{\frac{s(0,r)}{c_{v}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\qquad v(0,r)=0\qquad u(0,r)=q(0,r).

Next, the transport equations (
4.17
)-(
4.19
) become

(4.24)

\displaystyle\psi_{r}W_{z}-\psi_{z}W_{r}=0,\,\,\psi_{r}B_{z}-\psi_{z}B_{r}=0,\,\,\psi_{r}s_{z}-\psi_{z}s_{r}=0,

where
W:=rw
. For their initial conditions, we have

\displaystyle W(0,r)=

\displaystyle W_{EN}(r):=rw(0,r)

\displaystyle s(0,r)=

\displaystyle s_{EN}(r):=\bar{s}_{-}(r),

\displaystyle B(0,r)=

\displaystyle B_{EN}(r):=\frac{1}{2}\Big((q(0,r))^{2}+(w(0,r))^{2}\Big)+\frac{\gamma p(0,r)}{(\gamma-1)\rho(0,r)},

where the intial states are given by (
4.22
) and (
4.23
).

It follows from (
4.21
) and the properties that
\bar{\rho}\geq C>0
and
\bar{q}\geq C>0
that
\psi
is monotonely increasing with respect to
r\in[0,r_{0}]
. So it is one-to-one.
Let
\zeta:=\psi^{-1}
. Obviously,
\zeta
is a one to one mapping from
[0,\psi(r_{0})]
to
[0,r_{0}]
too.
Thus, (
4.24
) yields that for
\psi\in[0,\psi(r_{0})]
,

(4.25)

\displaystyle W(\psi)=W_{EN}(\zeta(\psi)),\quad B(\psi)=B_{EN}(\zeta(\psi)),\quad s(\psi)=s_{EN}(\zeta(\psi)).

Finally, let us consider equation (
4.16
). Let

(4.26)

\displaystyle F\big(\rho,B,s,\frac{W}{r},\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\big):=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}}+\rho^{2}(\frac{W}{r})^{2}\Big)+\frac{\gamma Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}\rho^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma-1}-\rho^{2}B.

Bernoulli’s law implies that
F\big(\rho,B,s,\frac{W}{r},\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)=0
. Taking the derivative on the both sides of (
4.26
) with respect to
\rho
, we have

\displaystyle F_{\rho}=

\displaystyle\rho((\frac{W}{r})^{2}-2B)+\frac{\gamma(\gamma+1)Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma(\gamma+1)Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}-\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}}-\frac{2\gamma Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rho}\big(c^{2}-(u^{2}+v^{2})\big)<0.

Thus, by the inverse function theorem, function
\rho
can be represented as a function of
(B,s,\frac{W}{r},\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}})
such that
F\big(\rho,B,s,\frac{W}{r},\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)=0
. That is,

(4.27)

\displaystyle\rho=\rho(B,s,\frac{W}{r},\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}}).

Notice that (
4.16
) is equivalent to

(4.28)

\displaystyle v(u_{r}-v_{z})=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}(u^{2}+v^{2})-\frac{1}{\rho}p_{z}.

Let

(4.29)

\displaystyle\psi=r^{2}\varphi.

Applying (
4.20
), direct calculations yield that

(4.30)

\displaystyle u_{r}-v_{z}=r\varphi_{rr}+r\varphi_{zz}+3\varphi_{r}-\frac{1}{\rho}(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})\rho_{r}-\frac{1}{\rho}r\varphi_{z}\rho_{z}.

Then let us calculate
\rho_{z}
and
\rho_{r}
. Let
\chi:=\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{r^{2}}
. It follows from (
4.26
) that

(4.31)

F_{B}=-\rho^{2}\qquad F_{s}=\frac{\gamma Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}\rho^{\gamma+1}}{(\gamma-1)c_{v}}=\frac{\gamma p\rho}{(\gamma-1)c_{v}}\qquad F_{\frac{W}{r}}=\rho^{2}\frac{W}{r}\qquad F_{\chi}=\frac{1}{2}.

Then by the Bernoulli’s law

\displaystyle F_{\rho}\rho_{z}=

\displaystyle F_{B}B_{z}+F_{s}s_{z}+F_{\frac{W}{r}}(\frac{W}{r})_{z}+F_{\chi}\chi_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(F_{B}B^{\prime}(\psi)+F_{s}s^{\prime}(\psi)+F_{\frac{W}{r}}\frac{W^{\prime}(\psi)}{r})\psi_{z}+\frac{1}{2r^{2}}\partial_{z}\Big(\psi_{z}^{2}+\psi_{r}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Big(\psi_{z}\psi_{zz}+\psi_{r}\psi_{zr}\Big)+\rho^{2}\left(-B^{\prime}+\frac{\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)c_{v}\rho}s^{\prime}+\frac{WW^{\prime}}{r^{2}}\right)\psi_{z}

\displaystyle F_{\rho}\rho_{r}=

\displaystyle F_{B}B_{r}+F_{s}s_{r}+F_{\frac{W}{r}}(\frac{W}{r})_{r}+F_{\chi}\chi_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(F_{B}B^{\prime}+F_{s}s^{\prime}+F_{\frac{W}{r}}\frac{W^{\prime}}{r})\psi_{r}-\frac{WF_{\frac{W}{r}}}{r^{2}}+\partial_{r}\Big(\frac{\psi_{z}^{2}+\psi_{r}^{2}}{2r^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Big(\psi_{z}\psi_{zr}+\psi_{r}\psi_{rr}\Big)-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\big(\psi_{z}^{2}+\psi_{r}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle+\rho^{2}\left(-B^{\prime}+\frac{\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)c_{v}\rho}s^{\prime}+\frac{WW^{\prime}}{r^{2}}\right)\psi_{r}-\frac{\rho^{2}W^{2}}{r^{3}}.

Note that
u^{2}+v^{2}=\frac{\chi}{\rho^{2}}
,
p=Ae^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}\rho^{\gamma}
and
F_{\rho}=\rho(c^{2}-(u^{2}+v^{2}))
. Then it follows from equations (
4.28
) and (
4.30
) and a straightforward and long computation that
\varphi
satisfies equation

\displaystyle\big(1-\frac{r^{2}\varphi_{z}^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)\varphi_{zz}+\big(1-\frac{(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)\big(\varphi_{rr}+\frac{3}{r}\varphi_{r}\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{2(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}r\varphi_{z}\varphi_{zr}-\frac{3(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\varphi_{z}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(\varphi,D\varphi)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(r\varphi_{r}+2\varphi)\Big\{\frac{\rho}{u}B^{\prime}(\psi)-\frac{\rho}{u}\frac{w}{r}W^{\prime}(\psi)-\frac{\rho^{\gamma}}{\gamma-1}\frac{A}{c_{v}}e^{\frac{s}{c_{v}}}\frac{1}{u}s^{\prime}(\psi)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\rho}(r\varphi_{r}+2\varphi)\Big(\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial B}B^{\prime}(\psi)+\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial s}s^{\prime}(\psi)+\frac{w}{r}\frac{\rho W^{\prime}(\psi)}{q^{2}-c^{2}}\Big)-\frac{1}{q^{2}-c^{2}}\frac{w^{2}}{r^{2}}\Big\}

(4.32)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\rho}\varphi_{z}\Big(\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial B}B^{\prime}(\psi)r^{2}\varphi_{z}+\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial s}s^{\prime}(\psi)r^{2}\varphi_{z}+\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial w}W^{\prime}(\psi)r\varphi_{z}\Big).

In order to overcome the difficulty from the singularity along the symmetry axis
\{r=0\}
in equation (
4.32
), we introduce
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})
and
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{4}r_{i}^{2}=r^{2}
. The domain
\Omega
becomes

\mathcal{D}_{-}:=\{(z,\mathbf{r}):z\in(0,L),\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{4},|\mathbf{r}|<r_{0}\}
.
Let

\phi(z,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})=\varphi(z,\sqrt{r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}+r_{3}^{2}+r_{4}^{2}}).

Then equation (
4.32
) becomes

\displaystyle\big(1-\frac{r^{2}\varphi_{z}^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)\phi_{zz}+\big(1-\frac{(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)\sum_{i=1}^{4}\phi_{r_{i}r_{i}}

(4.33)

\displaystyle-\frac{2(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\phi_{z}\sum_{i=1}^{4}r_{i}(\phi_{z})_{r_{i}}-\frac{3(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}(\phi_{z})^{2}=F(\phi,D\phi).

Next, at the background solution
\overline{U}_{-}
, it follows from (
4.20
) that

(4.34)

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}(r):=\psi(0,r)\equiv\int_{0}^{r}\tau\bar{\rho}_{-}(\tau)\bar{q}_{-}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau.

Then by definition (
4.29
), we have

(4.35)

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}(r)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\tau\bar{\rho}_{-}(\tau)\bar{q}_{-}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau.

Let

(4.36)

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}(z,r):=\varphi(z,r)-\overline{\varphi}(z,r),

and

\widehat{\phi}(z,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})=\widehat{\varphi}(z,\sqrt{r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}+r_{3}^{2}+r_{4}^{2}}).

Then it follows from (
4.33
) that
\widehat{\phi}
satisfies equation

\displaystyle\big(1-\frac{r^{2}\varphi_{z}^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)\widehat{\phi}_{zz}+\big(1-\frac{(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)\sum_{i=1}^{4}\widehat{\phi}_{r_{i}r_{i}}

\displaystyle-\frac{2(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\widehat{\phi}_{z}\sum_{i=1}^{4}r_{i}(\widehat{\phi}_{z})_{r_{i}}-\frac{3(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}(\widehat{\phi}_{z})^{2}

(4.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(\phi,D\phi)-\big(1-\frac{(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)\sum_{i=1}^{4}\overline{\phi}_{r_{i}r_{i}},

where
\overline{\phi}(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})=\overline{\varphi}(\sqrt{r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}+r_{3}^{2}+r_{4}^{2}})
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\big(1-\frac{r^{2}\varphi_{z}^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)=\big(1-\frac{\psi_{z}^{2}}{r^{2}\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)=\big(1-\frac{v^{2}}{q^{2}-c^{2}}\big)=\frac{u^{2}-c^{2}}{q^{2}-c^{2}},

\displaystyle\big(1-\frac{(2\varphi+r\varphi_{r})^{2}}{\rho^{2}(q^{2}-c^{2})}\big)=\big(1-\frac{u^{2}}{q^{2}-c^{2}}\big)=\frac{v^{2}-c^{2}}{q^{2}-c^{2}}.

Then equation (
4.37
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(u^{2}-c^{2})\widehat{\phi}_{zz}-(c^{2}-v^{2})\sum_{i=1}^{4}\widehat{\phi}_{r_{i}r_{i}}-\frac{2u}{\rho}\widehat{\phi}_{z}\sum_{i=1}^{4}r_{i}(\widehat{\phi}_{z})_{r_{i}}-\frac{3u}{\rho}(\widehat{\phi}_{z})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(q^{2}-c^{2})F+(c^{2}-v^{2})\sum_{i=1}^{4}\overline{\phi}_{r_{i}r_{i}}.

Recall that for the background solution,
\bar{v}=0
and
\bar{u}=\bar{q}>\bar{c}
. So for sufficiently small constant
\sigma>0
,
c^{2}-v^{2}>0
and
u^{2}-c^{2}>0
.
Thus, (
4.32
) is a five dimensional nonlinear wave equation.
By (
2.34
), (
2.37
), (
4.21
)-(
4.23
), (
4.29
) and (
4.35
)-(
4.36
), the initial-boundary conditions for
\widehat{\phi}
are

(4.38)

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(0,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})=

\displaystyle\frac{A^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\tau\bar{p}_{-}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(\tau)e^{-\frac{\bar{s}_{-}(\tau)}{\gamma c_{v}}}\sigma q_{en}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

(4.39)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\widehat{\phi}(0,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})=

\displaystyle 0

(4.40)

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(z,r_{0})=

\displaystyle 0,

where
r=\sqrt{r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}+r_{3}^{2}+r_{4}^{2}}
.

Let
\overline{H}(r):=\bar{p}_{-}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}(r)e^{-\frac{\bar{s}_{-}(r)}{\gamma c_{v}}}q_{en}(r)
, then (
4.38
) becomes

(4.41)

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(0,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})=\sigma\frac{A^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\tau\overline{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau.

Now we will show that

(4.42)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\tau\overline{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\in H^{7}(B_{r_{0}}).

First, by
\partial_{r}^{j}(\bar{p}_{-},\bar{s}_{-},q_{en})(0)=0
,
j=1,3,5
, we have

(4.43)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{j}\overline{H}(0)=0,\quad j=1,3,5.

Thus

(4.44)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}\partial_{r}\big(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\tau\overline{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\big)=\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}\big(\frac{r\overline{H}(r)}{r^{2}}-2\frac{\int_{0}^{r}\tau\overline{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau}{r^{3}}\big)=\frac{\partial_{r}\overline{H}(0)}{3}=0,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}\partial_{r}^{3}\big(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\tau\overline{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\big)=\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}\big(\frac{\overline{H}^{\prime\prime}(r)}{r}-4\frac{\overline{H}^{\prime}(r)}{r^{2}}+12\frac{\overline{H}(r)}{r^{3}}-24\frac{\int_{0}^{r}\tau\overline{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau}{r^{5}}\big)

(4.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\partial_{r}^{3}\overline{H}(0)=0,

(4.46)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}\partial_{r}^{5}\big(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}\tau\overline{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\big)=\frac{1}{7}\partial_{r}^{5}\overline{H}(0)=0.

By (
4.44
)-(
4.46
) and Lemma
4.1
, the initial data
\widehat{\phi}(0,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})\in H^{7}(B_{r_{0}})
.

By doing the standard energy estimate for the linear wave equation and the Picard iteration for the local existence (see Theorem 6.4.11 in
[
23
, Page 113]
), we have for any given
L>0
, if
\sigma>0
is sufficiently small, there is a unique solution
\widehat{\phi}\in\cap_{j=0}^{7}C^{j}\Big([0,L];H^{7-j}(\mathcal{D}_{-})\Big)
